People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess the main thing is… since I stopped working, I just don’t seem to know how to slow myself down. I’m 65 now, and I’m not working anymore, but my body doesn’t seem to have gotten the message. I’m up and around all day, going from one little job to the next—fixing this, straightening that, checking something, starting something else—like there’s still a crew waiting on me or a foreman coming around to see what’s been done.

It’s not even always big things, you know? It’s just constant movement. I’ll think, “I should sit down,” and then I notice something that needs doing, and then that leads to another thing, and pretty soon the whole day’s gone and I’ve barely actually rested. That’s really what brought me in. It’s wearing on me a bit, and I’m not sure why I can’t just ease into retirement like other people seem to.

I wouldn’t say it feels like worry exactly, no. It’s more like… I just can’t seem to shut the motor off. Since I stopped working, I’m still up and moving all day, bouncing from one little thing to the next, like I’ve got jobs lined up and somebody’s expecting me to get them done.

It’s not that I’m walking around feeling cheerful or excited, either. I don’t think I’m “upbeat” in that way. It’s more this constant push to be doing something. I’ll finish one small thing and then notice another, and before I know it I’m on to that. It reminds me of when I was working, like there’s still a crew waiting on me, even though there isn’t.

I’d say it’s really become noticeable since I stopped working. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say it’s made me low exactly, or that I can’t enjoy anything, but it has caused problems, yeah. More than I’d like to admit, probably.

My wife has had to kind of take over keeping the calendar for me, because I’ll say I’m going to do something or be somewhere, and then I get wrapped up in other little jobs and lose track. She’ll check behind me now—like, “Did you remember you told them you’d be there?” or “Did you call so-and-so back?” And I don’t love that. I mean, I’m grateful, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite managing things the way I should.

And I’ve had to apologize a few times, too. At the community center, and with the garden group I’m in, I’ve let people down because I missed something or didn’t follow through when I said I would. Nothing catastrophic, but enough that I feel embarrassed by it. So I guess it’s more than an inconvenience. It does bother me, mostly because other people end up having to compensate for me.

exactly, no. It’s more like… I just can’t seem to shut the motor off. I don’t think I’m “upbeat” in that way. It’s more this constant push to be doing something. I’ll finish one small thing and then notice another, and before I know it I’m on to that. It reminds me of when I was working, like there’s still a crew waiting on me, even though there isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Even years ago, before you retired, would you say you always had this same drive to stay busy and had trouble relaxing, or is this constant need to keep moving something that really only started once you stopped working?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s really become noticeable since I stopped working. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who likes having something to do, sure, but this particular thing—this feeling like I can’t slow myself down, like I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day—that’s what’s been standing out since retirement.\n\nWhen I was working, it sort of made sense, you know? There were actual jobs to get done, people depending on me, a schedule, a crew, all that. Now there isn’t, but I still find myself acting like there is. I’ll be home and I’ll notice one small thing, then another, and before I know it I’ve spent the whole day doing little tasks like I’m still on the clock. That’s the part that brought me in—like my body or my habits haven’t caught up with the fact that I’m not working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this constant need to stay busy is causing you real distress or upset—like feeling anxious, low, or overwhelmed—or is it more just an inconvenience that hasn't really affected your mood or ability to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s made me low exactly, or that I can’t enjoy anything, but it has caused problems, yeah. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy wife has had to kind of take over keeping the calendar for me, because I’ll say I’m going to do something or be somewhere, and then I get wrapped up in other little jobs and lose track. She’ll check behind me now—like, “Did you remember you told them you’d be there?” or “Did you call so-and-so back?” And I don’t love that. I mean, I’m grateful, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite managing things the way I should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize a few times, too. At the community center, and with the garden group I’m in, I’ve let people down because I missed something or didn’t follow through when I said I would. Nothing catastrophic, but enough that I feel embarrassed by it. So I guess it’s more than an inconvenience. It does bother me, mostly because other people end up having to compensate for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking back to your school years or early adulthood, did you often have trouble with forgetfulness, losing track of tasks, or staying organized, even before you had this recent urge to stay constantly busy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… thinking back, I guess that was there pretty early. I don’t know that I would’ve called it anything at the time, of course, but in grade school I remember hearing the same kind of comments over and over.

Teachers would tell my folks I was bright, that I knew the answers, but that I was scattered. That was the word, or something like it. I was always misplacing things—pencils especially, papers, whatever I was supposed to have right in front of me. And I’d get started on work and then somehow not finish it unless someone reminded me, like before recess the teacher would have to say, “You need to get that done first,” because I’d be halfway turned toward the door already.

I also remember calling out answers instead of waiting to be called on. Not trying to be rude, exactly—I just had the answer in my head and it would come out. So yes, I suppose the losing track and needing reminders part goes way back, even if I didn’t really think of it as a problem then.

before you retired, would you say you always had this same drive to stay busy and had trouble relaxing, or is this constant need to keep moving something that really only started once you stopped working?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s really become noticeable since I stopped working. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who likes having something to do, sure, but this particular thing—this feeling like I can’t slow myself down, like I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day—that’s what’s been standing out since retirement.\n\nWhen I was working, it sort of made sense, you know? There were actual jobs to get done, people depending on me, a schedule, a crew, all that. Now there isn’t, but I still find myself acting like there is. I’ll be home and I’ll notice one small thing, then another, and before I know it I’ve spent the whole day doing little tasks like I’m still on the clock. That’s the part that brought me in—like my body or my habits haven’t caught up with the fact that I’m not working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this constant need to stay busy is causing you real distress or upset—like feeling anxious, low, or overwhelmed—or is it more just an inconvenience that hasn't really affected your mood or ability to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s made me low exactly, or that I can’t enjoy anything, but it has caused problems, yeah. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy wife has had to kind of take over keeping the calendar for me, because I’ll say I’m going to do something or be somewhere, and then I get wrapped up in other little jobs and lose track. She’ll check behind me now—like, “Did you remember you told them you’d be there?” or “Did you call so-and-so back?” And I don’t love that. I mean, I’m grateful, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite managing things the way I should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize a few times, too. At the community center, and with the garden group I’m in, I’ve let people down because I missed something or didn’t follow through when I said I would. Nothing catastrophic, but enough that I feel embarrassed by it. So I guess it’s more than an inconvenience. It does bother me, mostly because other people end up having to compensate for me.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to your school years or early adulthood, did you often have trouble with forgetfulness, losing track of tasks, or staying organized, even before you had this recent urge to stay constantly busy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thinking back, I guess that was there pretty early. I don’t know that I would’ve called it anything at the time, of course, but in grade school I remember hearing the same kind of comments over and over.\n\nTeachers would tell my folks I was bright, that I knew the answers, but that I was scattered. That was the word, or something like it. I was always misplacing things—pencils especially, papers, whatever I was supposed to have right in front of me. And I’d get started on work and then somehow not finish it unless someone reminded me, like before recess the teacher would have to say, “You need to get that done first,” because I’d be halfway turned toward the door already.\n\nI also remember calling out answers instead of waiting to be called on. Not trying to be rude, exactly—I just had the answer in my head and it would come out. So yes, I suppose the losing track and needing reminders part goes way back, even if I didn’t really think of it as a problem then.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring all those working years, before you retired, did you notice similar issues with forgetfulness or losing track of tasks causing problems at work or home, or did that seem to fade once you had the structure of a job?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think the structure helped a lot when I was working. I mean, I won’t say I was perfect back then, but having a job and a routine kind of kept me pointed in the right direction. There were set times, set responsibilities, and other people around, so if I was supposed to do something, it was usually right there in front of me.

Now it shows up more in the day-to-day things. Like lately I’ll forget to return a call from the community center, even though I meant to do it as soon as I got home. Or I’ll set a bill aside thinking, “I’ll pay that after lunch,” and then my wife has to remind me because I’ve gone off and done five other things. Same with the garden group—I’ll tell them I’ll bring supplies, and then I’ll get there and realize I left them sitting by the door or never gathered them up in the first place.

So, I guess it didn’t exactly disappear when I was working, but the structure covered for it better. Since retirement, with everything looser, it’s easier for those things to slip through.

Not trying to be rude, exactly—I just had the answer in my head and it would come out. So yes, I suppose the losing track and needing reminders part goes way back, even if I didn’t really think of it as a problem then.\n\nDoctor: During all those working years, before you retired, did you notice similar issues with forgetfulness or losing track of tasks causing problems at work or home, or did that seem to fade once you had the structure of a job?\n\nPatient: I think the structure helped a lot when I was working. I mean, I won’t say I was perfect back then, but having a job and a routine kind of kept me pointed in the right direction. There were set times, set responsibilities, and other people around, so if I was supposed to do something, it was usually right there in front of me.\n\nNow it shows up more in the day-to-day things. Like lately I’ll forget to return a call from the community center, even though I meant to do it as soon as I got home. Or I’ll set a bill aside thinking, “I’ll pay that after lunch,” and then my wife has to remind me because I’ve gone off and done five other things. Same with the garden group—I’ll tell them I’ll bring supplies, and then I’ll get there and realize I left them sitting by the door or never gathered them up in the first place.\n\nSo, I guess it didn’t exactly disappear when I was working, but the structure covered for it better. Since retirement, with everything looser, it’s easier for those things to slip through.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do notice it, though maybe not in the same obvious way as when I was a kid blurting things out in class.

It’s more like… waiting just gets under my skin. If I’m standing in line at the hardware store and the person ahead of me is taking a long time, I’ll start shifting from one foot to the other, checking the time, looking around like that’s going to somehow make it move faster. Same thing at the clinic, sitting there waiting for my name to be called. I know perfectly well everyone has to wait their turn, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but inside I feel antsy, like I want to get up and push things along somehow.

I don’t usually say anything rude, at least I try not to, but my wife can tell. She’ll give me that look because I’m fidgeting or sighing or looking at the clock again. So yes, I guess the trouble waiting is still there—it’s just more contained than when I was younger.

I’ll think, “I should sit down,” and then I notice something that needs doing, and then that leads to another thing, and pretty soon the whole day’s gone and I’ve barely actually rested. That’s really what brought me in. It’s wearing on me a bit, and I’m not sure why I can’t just ease into retirement like other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: When you're moving from task to task like that, would you say you're feeling worried or on edge, or do you actually feel unusually energetic, upbeat, or 'wired' inside?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it feels like worry exactly, no. It’s more like… I just can’t seem to shut the motor off. Since I stopped working, I’m still up and moving all day, bouncing from one little thing to the next, like I’ve got jobs lined up and somebody’s expecting me to get them done.\n\nIt’s not that I’m walking around feeling cheerful or excited, either. I don’t think I’m “upbeat” in that way. It’s more this constant push to be doing something. I’ll finish one small thing and then notice another, and before I know it I’m on to that. It reminds me of when I was working, like there’s still a crew waiting on me, even though there isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Even years ago, before you retired, would you say you always had this same drive to stay busy and had trouble relaxing, or is this constant need to keep moving something that really only started once you stopped working?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s really become noticeable since I stopped working. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who likes having something to do, sure, but this particular thing—this feeling like I can’t slow myself down, like I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day—that’s what’s been standing out since retirement.\n\nWhen I was working, it sort of made sense, you know? There were actual jobs to get done, people depending on me, a schedule, a crew, all that. Now there isn’t, but I still find myself acting like there is. I’ll be home and I’ll notice one small thing, then another, and before I know it I’ve spent the whole day doing little tasks like I’m still on the clock. That’s the part that brought me in—like my body or my habits haven’t caught up with the fact that I’m not working anymore.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this constant need to stay busy is causing you real distress or upset—like feeling anxious, low, or overwhelmed—or is it more just an inconvenience that hasn't really affected your mood or ability to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s made me low exactly, or that I can’t enjoy anything, but it has caused problems, yeah. More than I’d like to admit, probably.\n\nMy wife has had to kind of take over keeping the calendar for me, because I’ll say I’m going to do something or be somewhere, and then I get wrapped up in other little jobs and lose track. She’ll check behind me now—like, “Did you remember you told them you’d be there?” or “Did you call so-and-so back?” And I don’t love that. I mean, I’m grateful, but it makes me feel like I’m not quite managing things the way I should.\n\nAnd I’ve had to apologize a few times, too. At the community center, and with the garden group I’m in, I’ve let people down because I missed something or didn’t follow through when I said I would. Nothing catastrophic, but enough that I feel embarrassed by it. So I guess it’s more than an inconvenience. It does bother me, mostly because other people end up having to compensate for me.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to your school years or early adulthood, did you often have trouble with forgetfulness, losing track of tasks, or staying organized, even before you had this recent urge to stay constantly busy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thinking back, I guess that was there pretty early. I don’t know that I would’ve called it anything at the time, of course, but in grade school I remember hearing the same kind of comments over and over.\n\nTeachers would tell my folks I was bright, that I knew the answers, but that I was scattered. That was the word, or something like it. I was always misplacing things—pencils especially, papers, whatever I was supposed to have right in front of me. And I’d get started on work and then somehow not finish it unless someone reminded me, like before recess the teacher would have to say, “You need to get that done first,” because I’d be halfway turned toward the door already.\n\nI also remember calling out answers instead of waiting to be called on. Not trying to be rude, exactly—I just had the answer in my head and it would come out. So yes, I suppose the losing track and needing reminders part goes way back, even if I didn’t really think of it as a problem then.\n\nDoctor: During all those working years, before you retired, did you notice similar issues with forgetfulness or losing track of tasks causing problems at work or home, or did that seem to fade once you had the structure of a job?\n\nPatient: I think the structure helped a lot when I was working. I mean, I won’t say I was perfect back then, but having a job and a routine kind of kept me pointed in the right direction. There were set times, set responsibilities, and other people around, so if I was supposed to do something, it was usually right there in front of me.\n\nNow it shows up more in the day-to-day things. Like lately I’ll forget to return a call from the community center, even though I meant to do it as soon as I got home. Or I’ll set a bill aside thinking, “I’ll pay that after lunch,” and then my wife has to remind me because I’ve gone off and done five other things. Same with the garden group—I’ll tell them I’ll bring supplies, and then I’ll get there and realize I left them sitting by the door or never gathered them up in the first place.\n\nSo, I guess it didn’t exactly disappear when I was working, but the structure covered for it better. Since retirement, with everything looser, it’s easier for those things to slip through.\n\nDoctor: Do you still notice yourself interrupting people in conversation or having trouble waiting your turn, like in lines or group discussions, the way you did as a child calling out answers?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice it, though maybe not in the same obvious way as when I was a kid blurting things out in class.\n\nIt’s more like… waiting just gets under my skin. If I’m standing in line at the hardware store and the person ahead of me is taking a long time, I’ll start shifting from one foot to the other, checking the time, looking around like that’s going to somehow make it move faster. Same thing at the clinic, sitting there waiting for my name to be called. I know perfectly well everyone has to wait their turn, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but inside I feel antsy, like I want to get up and push things along somehow.\n\nI don’t usually say anything rude, at least I try not to, but my wife can tell. She’ll give me that look because I’m fidgeting or sighing or looking at the clock again. So yes, I guess the trouble waiting is still there—it’s just more contained than when I was younger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, my family noticed it too, mostly because the teachers kept bringing it up and then my parents would see the same kind of thing at home. I remember it being this regular theme, like, “You’re a smart boy, but you’re so scattered.” That word stuck with me.

At home it would be things like losing pencils or school papers, or leaving something somewhere and then having no idea where it went. And finishing things was an issue too. I’d start something for school and then drift off unless somebody reminded me. The teacher would have to remind me to finish my work before recess, and I think at home my parents saw that same pattern—needing to be brought back to the task.

And the calling out answers, that was more at school, but my folks definitely heard about it. It wasn’t that I meant to misbehave. I just had the answer in my head and out it came. So yes, it wasn’t only school noticing. My parents knew I was bright, but they also knew I had trouble keeping track of things and following through without reminders.

I don’t know that I would’ve called it anything at the time, of course, but in grade school I remember hearing the same kind of comments over and over.\n\nTeachers would tell my folks I was bright, that I knew the answers, but that I was scattered. That was the word, or something like it. I was always misplacing things—pencils especially, papers, whatever I was supposed to have right in front of me. And I’d get started on work and then somehow not finish it unless someone reminded me, like before recess the teacher would have to say, “You need to get that done first,” because I’d be halfway turned toward the door already.\n\nI also remember calling out answers instead of waiting to be called on. Not trying to be rude, exactly—I just had the answer in my head and it would come out. So yes, I suppose the losing track and needing reminders part goes way back, even if I didn’t really think of it as a problem then.\n\nDoctor: During all those working years, before you retired, did you notice similar issues with forgetfulness or losing track of tasks causing problems at work or home, or did that seem to fade once you had the structure of a job?\n\nPatient: I think the structure helped a lot when I was working. I mean, I won’t say I was perfect back then, but having a job and a routine kind of kept me pointed in the right direction. There were set times, set responsibilities, and other people around, so if I was supposed to do something, it was usually right there in front of me.\n\nNow it shows up more in the day-to-day things. Like lately I’ll forget to return a call from the community center, even though I meant to do it as soon as I got home. Or I’ll set a bill aside thinking, “I’ll pay that after lunch,” and then my wife has to remind me because I’ve gone off and done five other things. Same with the garden group—I’ll tell them I’ll bring supplies, and then I’ll get there and realize I left them sitting by the door or never gathered them up in the first place.\n\nSo, I guess it didn’t exactly disappear when I was working, but the structure covered for it better. Since retirement, with everything looser, it’s easier for those things to slip through.\n\nDoctor: Do you still notice yourself interrupting people in conversation or having trouble waiting your turn, like in lines or group discussions, the way you did as a child calling out answers?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do notice it, though maybe not in the same obvious way as when I was a kid blurting things out in class.\n\nIt’s more like… waiting just gets under my skin. If I’m standing in line at the hardware store and the person ahead of me is taking a long time, I’ll start shifting from one foot to the other, checking the time, looking around like that’s going to somehow make it move faster. Same thing at the clinic, sitting there waiting for my name to be called. I know perfectly well everyone has to wait their turn, and I’m not trying to be difficult, but inside I feel antsy, like I want to get up and push things along somehow.\n\nI don’t usually say anything rude, at least I try not to, but my wife can tell. She’ll give me that look because I’m fidgeting or sighing or looking at the clock again. So yes, I guess the trouble waiting is still there—it’s just more contained than when I was younger.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did your parents or family also notice these same problems—like forgetfulness, losing things, or trouble finishing tasks—causing issues at home, not just at school?\n\nPatient: Yes, my family noticed it too, mostly because the teachers kept bringing it up and then my parents would see the same kind of thing at home. I remember it being this regular theme, like, “You’re a smart boy, but you’re so scattered.” That word stuck with me.\n\nAt home it would be things like losing pencils or school papers, or leaving something somewhere and then having no idea where it went. And finishing things was an issue too. I’d start something for school and then drift off unless somebody reminded me. The teacher would have to remind me to finish my work before recess, and I think at home my parents saw that same pattern—needing to be brought back to the task.\n\nAnd the calling out answers, that was more at school, but my folks definitely heard about it. It wasn’t that I meant to misbehave. I just had the answer in my head and out it came. So yes, it wasn’t only school noticing. My parents knew I was bright, but they also knew I had trouble keeping track of things and following through without reminders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No evidence supporting mania/hypomania, anxiety disorder, or dementia-related cognitive decline.", "is_final": true}

Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S014\",\"name\":\"Constant_Activity\",\"description\":\"Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so I’m returning the chief complaint symptom code as instructed.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’d say it’s really become noticeable since I stopped working. I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who likes having something to do, sure, but this particular thing—this feeling like I can’t slow myself down, like I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day—that’s what’s been standing out since retirement.\n\nWhen I was working, it sort of made sense, you know? There were actual jobs to get done, people depending on me, a schedule, a crew, all that. Now there isn’t, but I still find myself acting like there is. I’ll be home and I’ll notice one small thing, then another, and before I know it I’ve spent the whole day doing little tasks like I’m still on the clock. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
